[A discourse] spoken by way of indication, without announcing a person's name and lineage — [but] proclaiming [it], spoken for the continuance in time of the true dharma and of the teaching.
sbh1.3:57.3
iyamucyate udānā |
This is called udāna.
sbh1.3:58.1
nidānaṁ katamat |
What is nidāna?
sbh1.3:58.2
yatpudgalasya nāmagotramparikīrtayitvā kīrttya uddiśya bhāṣitam |
[A discourse] spoken by way of indication, with a person's name and lineage announced;
sbh1.3:58.3
yacca vinayapratisaṁyuktaṁ sotpattikaṁ sanidānaṁ prātimokṣasūtramidamucyate nidānaṁ |
and the vinaya-connected Prātimokṣa-sūtra with its origin and occasioning cause — this is called nidāna.
sbh1.3:58.4
avadānaṁ katamat |
What is avadāna?
sbh1.3:58.5
yatsadṛṣṭāntakamudāhataṁ |
[A discourse] given with a parable,
sbh1.3:58.6
yena dṛṣṭāntena yasya prakṛtasyārthasya vyavadānaṁ bhavatīdamucyate |
by which parable the present matter is illuminated — this is called
sbh1.3:58.7
avadānaṁ |
avadāna.
sbh1.3:58.8
vṛttaṁ katamat |
What is vṛttaka (itivṛttaka)?
sbh1.3:58.9
yatkiṁcitpūrvayogapratisaṁyuktamidamucyate vṛttakaṁ |
Whatever is connected with past undertakings (pūrva-yoga) — this is called vṛttaka.
sbh1.3:59.1
jātakaṁ katamat |
What is jātaka?
sbh1.3:59.2
yadatītamadhvānamupādāya tatra tatra bhagavataḥ cyutyupapādeṣu bodhisattvacaryā duṣkaracaryā |
Where the bodhisattva-conducts and difficult deeds of the Blessed One, in his successive births in the past, here and there,
sbh1.3:59.3
ākhyātā |
are recounted —
sbh1.3:59.4
idamucyate jātakaṁ |
this is called jātaka.
sbh1.3:60.1
vaipulyaṁ katamat |
What is vaipulya?
sbh1.3:60.2
yatra bodhisattvānāṁ mārgo deśyate |
Where the bodhisattvas' path is taught —
sbh1.3:60.3
anuttarāyai samyak saṁbodhaye |
for supreme, perfect awakening,
sbh1.3:60.4
daśabalānāvaraṇajñānasamudāgamāya |
for the perfecting of the ten powers and the unobstructed knowledge —
sbh1.3:60.5
idamucyate vaipulyaṁ |
this is called vaipulya.
sbh1.3:61.1
adbhutādharmāḥ katame |
What are the wonderful dharmas?
sbh1.3:61.2
yatra buddhānāñca, buddhaśrāvakāṇāṁ ca, bhikṣūṇāñca, bhikṣuṇīnāñca, śikṣamāṇānāṁ, śrāmaṇekī rī ṇāmupāsikānāṁ, sādhāraṇāsādhāraṇāśca tadanyaprativiśiṣṭāścāścaryādbhutasammatā guṇaviśeṣā ākhyātā ime ucyante adbhutā dharmāḥ |
Where the special qualities of the Buddhas, the disciples of Buddhas, monks, nuns, female probationers, male novices, female novices, male lay-followers, [and] female lay-followers — both their shared qualities and the distinctive qualities particular to each — are declared as wondrous and marvelous, these are called wonderful dharmas.
The adbhuta-dharma audience list is restored as the standard fourfold-plus-training community: monks, nuns, female probationers, male and female novices, male and female lay-followers. The extracted root is compressed/noisy around śrāmaṇera/śrāmaṇerī/upāsaka/upāsikā, but omitting female novices and male lay-followers would make the community list asymmetrical.
sbh1.3:62.1
upadeśāḥ katame |
What are upadeśas?
sbh1.3:62.2
sarvamātṛkā abhidharmaḥ sūtrāntaniṣkarṣaḥ |
All the mātṛkās, the abhidharma, and the digests drawn from the sūtras.
sbh1.3:62.3
sūtrāntavyākhyānamupadeśa ityucyate |
Sūtra-commentary is called upadeśa.
sbh1.3:62.4
taccaitad dvādaśāṁgavacogatamasti sūtramasti vinayaḥ, astyabhidharmaḥ |
And this twelvefold word-genre [maps to the three baskets]: there is sūtra, there is vinaya, there is abhidharma.
The mapping of the twelve aṅgas (verbal genres) onto the three piṭakas (baskets) given here is a Yogācāra schema: ten aṅgas (sūtra through adbhutadharma) constitute the sūtra-piṭaka; nidāna alone constitutes the vinaya-piṭaka (since vinaya rules are framed by their occasioning narratives); upadeśa constitutes the abhidharma-piṭaka. Different traditions (Theravāda, etc.) assign these differently.
sbh1.3:62.5
tatra yattāvadāha |
When [the source] first says:
sbh1.3:62.6
sūtraṁ geyaṁ vyākaraṇaṁ gāthodānāvadānavṛttakajātakavaipulyādbhutadharmā iti |
‘sūtra, geya, vyākaraṇa, gāthā, udāna, avadāna, vṛttaka, jātaka, vaipulya, wonderful dharmas’ —
sbh1.3:62.7
idaṁ tāvatsūtraṁ |
this [group of ten] is the sūtra-piṭaka.
sbh1.3:62.8
yatpunarāha |
When it then says:
sbh1.3:62.9
nidānamityayamucyate |
‘nidāna’ — this is called
sbh1.3:62.10
vinayaḥ |
the vinaya-piṭaka.
sbh1.3:62.11
yatpunarāha |
When it then says:
sbh1.3:62.12
upadeśā iti |
‘upadeśas’ —
sbh1.3:62.13
ayamucyate |
this is called
sbh1.3:62.14
abhidharmaḥ |
the abhidharma-piṭaka.
sbh1.3:62.15
taccaitad dvādaśāṁ gaṁ śru taṁ piṭakatrayasaṁgṛhītaṁ |
This twelvefold scripture, subsumed under the three baskets,
sbh1.3:62.16
sadbhiḥ samyaggatairdeśitaṁ |
taught by the rightly-gone good persons,
sbh1.3:62.17
saddharma ityucyate |
is called the true dharma.
sbh1.3:62.18
tasya śravaṇaṁ |
And the hearing of it is
sbh1.3:62.19
saddharmaśravaṇaṁ |
the hearing of the true dharma.
sbh1.3:62.20
tatpunaḥ katamat |
And what again is hearing?
sbh1.3:62.21
yathāpīhaikatyaḥ sūtradharo vā bhavati |
When someone here is a sūtra-bearer (sūtra-dhara),
sbh1.3:62.22
vinayadharo vā |
or a vinaya-bearer,
sbh1.3:62.23
mātṛkādharo vā, sūtravinayadharo vā, sūtrābhidharmadharo vā, vinayamātṛkādharo vā |
or a mātṛkā-bearer, or a sūtra-vinaya-bearer, or a sūtra-abhidharma-bearer, or a vinaya-mātṛkā-bearer —
sbh1.3:62.24
idamucyate saddharmaśravaṇaṁ |
this is called hearing of the true dharma.
sbh1.3:62.25
tatpunaḥ |
And that hearing,
sbh1.3:62.26
śravaṇaṁ dvividhaṁ |
again, is twofold:
sbh1.3:62.27
vyaṁjanaśrama va ṇamarthaśravaṇaṁ ca |
hearing of the letter and hearing of the meaning.
sbh1.3:62.28
cintanā katamā |
What is reflection?
sbh1.3:62.29
yathāpīhaikatyastāneva yathāśrutāndharmānekākī rahogataḥ |
When someone here, alone and secluded, [takes up] those very dharmas as heard —
sbh1.3:62.30
ṣaḍacintyāni sthānānitadyathā, ātmacintāṁ, sattvacintāṁ, lokacintāṁ, satvā ttvā nāṁ karmavipākacintāṁ, dhyāyināṁ dhyāyiviṣayaṁ, buddhānāṁ buddhaviṣayaṁ varjayitvā svalakṣaṇataḥ |
having set aside the six matters that are unthinkable (namely: reflection on self, on the being, on the world, on the karmic-results of beings, on the meditator's domain accessible only to meditators, and on the Buddha's domain accessible only to Buddhas) — he reflects on them by their own characteristic
The six acintya-sthāna (unthinkable matters) — self, being, world, the karmic results of beings, the meditator's range as known to meditators, and the Buddha's range as known to Buddhas — are explicitly set aside before reflection begins. The reflection that constitutes cintanā operates on what can be analytically known: dharma's own-characteristic (svalakṣaṇa) and general-characteristic (sāmānya-lakṣaṇa).
sbh1.3:62.31
sāmānyalakṣaṇataśca cintayati |
and by their general characteristic.
sbh1.3:62.32
sā punaḥ cintā dvividhā gaṇanākārāsaha gaṇanāyogena dharmeṇa |
That reflection, again, is twofold: by way of enumeration (joined with the enumeration-procedure for dharma),
sbh1.3:62.33
tulanākārama rā , yuktyā guṇadoṣaparīkṣaṇākārā ca |
and by way of weighing (by reasoning, examining merits and faults).
sbh1.3:62.34
sa cetskandhapratisaṁyuktāṁ deśanāṁ cintayati |
If he reflects on the aggregate-connected teaching.
sbh1.3:62.35
sa cedanyatamānyatamānyatamāṁ pūrvaniviṣṭāṁ deśanāṁ cintayatyābhyāṁ cintayati |
If he reflects on some particular previously-settled teaching, by these two he reflects.
sbh1.3:62.36
yathā punaḥ kathami tirūpamucyate |
How again is form said?
sbh1.3:62.37
daśa rūpīṇyāyatanānīti |
‘Ten form-sense-bases.’
sbh1.3:62.38
yacca dharmāyatanaparyāpannaṁ rūpaṁ sa ca rūpaskandhaḥ, tisro vedanā vedanāskandhaḥ |
And that form included in the dharma-sense-base — that is the form-aggregate; the three feelings are the feeling-aggregate.
sbh1.3:62.39
ṣaṭ saṁjñākāyāḥ saṁjñāskandhaḥ |
The six perception-bodies are the perception-aggregate.
sbh1.3:62.40
ṣaṭ cetanākāyāḥ cetanāskandhaḥ |
The six bodies of volition are the formations-aggregate.
sbh1.3:62.41
ṣaḍ vijñānakāyā vijñānaskandha ityevaṁ gaṇanāsaṁkhyākārāṁ skandha gaṇanāṁ cintayatyuttarottaraprabhedena yena vā punarasyāḥ saṁkhyāgaṇanā kārāyāścintāyā apramāṇaḥ praveśanayo veditavyaḥ |
The six consciousness-groups are the consciousness-aggregate. Thus, by way of enumeration-and-counting, he reflects on the aggregates with progressive subdivision; the entry-procedure for this enumeration-mode of reflection is to be understood as boundless.
sbh1.3:62.42
kathaṁ yuktyupaparīkṣākārayā cintayā skandhadeśanāṁ cintayati |
How does he reflect on the aggregate-teaching by way of examining reasoning?
sbh1.3:62.43
catasṛbhiryuktibhirupaparīkṣate |
He examines by four reasonings.
sbh1.3:62.44
katamābhiścatasṛbhiryadutāpekṣāyuktyā, kāryakāraṇayuktyā, upapattisādhanayuktyā |
Which four? Namely: the reasoning of dependence, the reasoning of cause-and-effect, the reasoning of demonstration-of-arising,
sbh1.3:62.45
dharmatāyuktyā |
and the reasoning of dharma-nature.
sbh1.3:63.1
apekṣāyuktiḥ katamā |
What is the reasoning of dependence?
sbh1.3:63.2
dvividhā apekṣā utpatyapekṣā prajñaptyapekṣā ca |
Dependence is twofold: dependence-of-arising (utpatty-apekṣā) and dependence-of-designation (prajñapty-apekṣā).
The four reasonings (yukti) introduced at 62.44 — apekṣā (dependence), kāryakāraṇa (cause-effect), upapatti-sādhana (demonstration-of-arising), dharmatā (dharma-nature) — are a canonical Yogācāra fourfold, expanded in section 63. Compare the four-reasoning treatment in [sbh3.2](sbh3.2.html#sbh3.2:0.2) (vipaśyanā), where they reappear as the analytical engines of insight.
sbh1.3:63.3
tatrotpattyapekṣā yairhetupratyayaiḥ skandhānāṁ prādurbhāvo bhavati |
Of these, dependence-of-arising: the causes and conditions on which the manifestation of the aggregates depends.
sbh1.3:63.4
tasyāṁ skandhotpattau te hetavaste, pratyayā apekṣyante |
In that arising of the aggregates, those causes and conditions are depended upon.
sbh1.3:63.5
yairnāmakāyapadakāyavyaṁjanakāyaiḥ skandhānāṁ prajñaptirbhavati |
[Dependence-of-designation:] the sequences of names, words, and letters on which the designation of the aggregates depends.
sbh1.3:63.6
tasyāṁ skandhaprajñaptau te nāmapadakāyavyaṁjanakāyā apekṣyante |
In that designation of the aggregates, those sequences of names, words, and letters are depended upon.
sbh1.3:63.7
iyamucyate skandheṣūtpattyapekṣā |
This is called dependence-of-arising in the aggregates
sbh1.3:63.8
prajñaptyapekṣatā kṣā ca |
and dependence-of-designation.
sbh1.3:63.9
yā cotpattyapekṣā |
Whichever is the dependence-of-arising,
sbh1.3:63.10
yā ca prajñaptyapekṣā sā yuktiryoga upāyaḥ |
and whichever is the dependence-of-designation — that is the reasoning, the procedure, the means
sbh1.3:63.11
skandhotpattaye |
for [the practitioner's grasp of] aggregate-arising
sbh1.3:63.12
skandhaprajñaptaye tasmādapekṣāyuktirityucyate |
and aggregate-designation; therefore it is called the reasoning of dependence.
sbh1.3:63.13
kāryakāraṇayuktiryā ta dutpannānāṁ skandhānāṁ svena hetunā svena pratyaryena tasmiṁstasmin svakāryakaraṇe viniyogastadyathā |
Reasoning of cause-and-effect: the engagement of the so-arisen aggregates, by means of their own cause and their own condition, in performing their respective proper functions — for example:
sbh1.3:63.14
cakṣuṣā rūpāṇi draṣṭavyāni |
by the eye, forms are to be seen;
sbh1.3:63.15
śrotreṇa śabdā ḥ śrotavyāḥ |
by the ear, sounds are to be heard;
sbh1.3:63.16
yāvanmanasā dharmā vijñeyā iti |
and so on up to: by the mind, dharmas are to be cognized.
sbh1.3:63.17
rūpeṇa cakṣuṣo gocare avasthātavyaṁ |
[Conversely:] form is to stand in the eye's range;
sbh1.3:63.18
śabdena śrotrasya, evaṁ yāddharmairmanasa iti |
sound in the ear's range; and so on through dharmas in the mind's range —
sbh1.3:63.19
yadvā punaranyadapyevaṁbhāgīyaṁ |
and whatever else is of this kind.
sbh1.3:63.20
tatra tatra dharmāṇāmanyo a nyaṁ kāryakāraṇe pratiyuktiryoga upāya iyamucyate |
Wherever, among the dharmas, the mutual cause-and-effect counterparts thus apply — that procedure, that means — is called
sbh1.3:63.21
kāryakāraṇayuktiḥ |
reasoning of cause-and-effect.
sbh1.3:63.22
upapattisādhanayuktiḥ katamā |
What is the reasoning of demonstration-of-arising?
sbh1.3:63.23
anityā ḥ skandhā iti, pratītyasamutpannā, duḥkhā ḥ , śūnyā, anātmāna iti tribhiḥ pramāṇairupaparīkṣate yadu ta tāptāgamena, pratyakṣeṇānumānena ca |
[The propositions:] ‘the aggregates are impermanent, dependently arisen, suffering, empty, non-self’ — these he examines by three means of valid cognition: namely, reliable scripture, direct perception, and inference.
sbh1.3:63.24
ebhistribhiḥ pramāṇairūpapattiyuktaiḥ satāṁ hṛdayagrāhakairvyavasthāpanā sādhanā kriyate |
By these three demonstration-yoked means of valid cognition, which grip the hearts of the wise, delineation and demonstration are made —
sbh1.3:63.25
yaduta skandhānityatāyā vā, pratītyasamutpannatāyā vā, duḥkhatāyā ḥ , śūnyatāyā, iyamucyate upapattisādhanayuktiḥ |
namely, of the impermanence, dependent-arising, suffering, emptiness, [and non-self] of the aggregates. This is called the reasoning of demonstration-of-arising.
The proof list at 63.23 names five characteristics of the aggregates: impermanent, dependently arisen, suffering, empty, and non-self. The summary at 63.25 explicitly includes impermanence, dependent-arising, suffering, and emptiness; non-self is supplied in English to keep the summary aligned with the proposition list immediately above.
sbh1.3:63.26
dharmatāyuktiḥ katamā |
What is the reasoning of dharma-nature?
sbh1.3:63.27
kena kāraṇena tathābhūtā ete skandhā ḥ , tathābhūto lokasanniveśaḥ kena kāraṇena kharalakṣaṇā pṛthivī, dravalakṣaṇā āpaḥ, uṣṇalakṣaṇaṁ tejaḥ ḥ , samudīraṇalakṣaṇo vāyuḥ, |
By what reason are these aggregates as they are, the world-arrangement as it is? By what reason is earth marked by hardness, water by fluidity, fire by heat, wind by motion?
sbh1.3:63.28
anityāḥ, skandhā ḥ , kena kāraṇena śāntaṁ nirvāṇamiti |
[By what reason are] the aggregates impermanent, [and] by what reason is nirvāṇa peaceful?
sbh1.3:63.29
tathā rūpaṇalakṣaṇaṁ rūpaṁ |
[Likewise:] form is marked by being-formed;
sbh1.3:63.30
anubhavalakṣaṇā vedanā, saṁjānanalakṣaṇā saṁjñā, abhisaṁskaraṇalakṣaṇāḥ saṁskārāḥ, vijānanālakṣaṇaṁ vijñānamiti |
feeling by experiencing, perception by conceiving, formations by forming, consciousness by cognizing.
sbh1.3:63.31
prakṛtireṣāṁ dharmāṇāmiyaṁ svabhāva eṣa īdṛśaḥ |
This is the nature of these dharmas; their own-nature is just this.
sbh1.3:63.32
dharmataiṣā caiva cāsau dharmatā |
This is dharma-nature, and this very dharma-nature itself
sbh1.3:63.33
saivātra yuktiryoga upāyaḥ |
is the reasoning, the procedure, the means here.
sbh1.3:63.34
evaṁ vā etat syāt |
[Whether one asks:] Could this be so,
sbh1.3:63.35
anyathā vā, naiva vā syāt, sarvatraiva ca dharmataiva pratiprasaraṇadharmataiva yuktiḥ |
or otherwise, or not at all — in every case, dharma-nature alone is the reasoning, [the explanation that] falls back on dharma-nature itself.
sbh1.3:63.36
cittanidhyāyanāya, cittasaṁjñāpanāya iyamucyate dharmatāyuktiḥ |
[It serves] for the mind to contemplate, for the mind to assent — this is called the reasoning of dharma-nature.
sbh1.3:63.37
evaṁ catasṛbhiryuktabhiḥ skandhadeśanā upaparīkṣyata iti |
Thus by these four reasonings the aggregate-teaching is examined.
sbh1.3:63.38
yāvatpunaranyā kāciddeśanā iti yā evamābhyāṁ dvābhyāmākārābhyāṁ gaṇanāsaṁkhyākārā ca yuktyupaparīkṣaṇākārā ca samyagupanidhyāyanā tasyāstasyā deśanāyāiyamucyate |
And whatever other teaching too — when, by these two modes (enumeration and examining-by-reasoning) it is rightly contemplated — that teaching's [contemplation] is called
sbh1.3:63.39
saddharmaśravaṇacinta n ā |
hearing-and-reflection on the true dharma.
sbh1.3:64.1
anantarāyaḥ katamaḥ |
What is non-obstacle?
sbh1.3:64.2
anantarāyo dvividhaḥ |
Non-obstacle is twofold:
sbh1.3:64.3
adhyātmamupādāya bahirdhā ca |
the inward and the outward.
sbh1.3:64.4
tatrādhyātmaṁ bahirdhā copādāyāntarāya yaṁ vakṣyati |
What follows will state the inward and outward obstacles;
anantarāya is defined here by negation: the discussion (64.7–64.30) details the obstacles (antarāya); non-obstacle is then implied as their absence. This negative-definition pattern is common in the Śrāvakabhūmi when the positive content has already been laid out elsewhere or is best understood by contrast.
sbh1.3:64.5
tadviparyayeṇa |
and by their reverse,
sbh1.3:64.6
nantarāyo veditavyaḥ |
non-obstacle is to be understood.
sbh1.3:64.7
adhyātmamupādāyāntarāyaḥ katamaḥ |
What, then, is the inward obstacle?
sbh1.3:64.8
yathāpīhaikatyaḥ pūrvameva kṛtapuṇyo bhavati |
When someone has previously made merit,
sbh1.3:64.9
saukṛtatvāt puṇyānāṁ ca lābhī bhavati |
and by the well-doneness of those merits is now a gainer
sbh1.3:64.10
kālena kālamānulomikāṁ kānāṁ, keṣu jīvitapariṣkārāṇāṁ reṣu |
— from time to time — of life-conforming requisites,
sbh1.3:64.11
yaduta cīvarapiṇḍapātaśayanāsanaglānapratyayabhaiṣajyapariṣkārāṇāṁ reṣu tīvrarāgo bhavatyāyata na rāgaḥ |
namely robes, alms-food, lodging, medicine, and provisions: in the very midst of these, intense and prolonged lust arises,
sbh1.3:64.12
tīvradveṣaḥ āyata na dveṣaḥ |
intense and prolonged hatred,
sbh1.3:64.13
tīvramoha āyata na mohaḥ |
intense and prolonged delusion.
sbh1.3:64.14
pūrvaṁ vānenābādhasaṁvarttanīyāni karmāṇi kṛtāni bhavanti |
Or, again, he has previously committed actions that lead to illness,
sbh1.3:64.15
yaddhetorābādhabahulo bhavati |
by which he is now full of illness —
sbh1.3:64.16
dṛṣṭa eva dharme viṣamacārī bhavati |
in this very life he moves about in an unbalanced way;
sbh1.3:64.17
yo ye nāsyābhīkṣṇaṁ vāto vā kupyati, pittamvā, śleṣmamvā, viṣūcikā vā kāye santiṣṭhate |
his wind, bile, phlegm, or cholera frequently rises and persists in his body.
sbh1.3:64.18
bhojanaguruko bhavati |
He becomes [over-]heavy in food,
sbh1.3:64.19
bahvartho, bahukṛtyo, gaṇasannipātabahulo bhavati |
of much business, much to do, frequently in large gatherings.
sbh1.3:64.20
karmārāmo vā, bhāṣyārāmo vā, nidrārāgaḥ maḥ , saṁgaṇikārāmaḥ |
Or fond of work, fond of speech, fond of sleep, fond of company,
sbh1.3:64.21
ātmasaṁpragrāhakaścapalaḥ, pramattaḥ kudeśavāsī vā |
or self-aggrandizing, fickle, heedless, dwelling in a bad place.
sbh1.3:64.22
ityevaṁbhāgīyā antarāyā adhyātmamupādāya veditavyāḥ |
Such are the inward obstacles, to be understood.
sbh1.3:64.23
bahirdhopādāyāntarāyāḥ katame |
What are the outward obstacles?
sbh1.3:64.24
tathāpi tadasatpuruṣāpāśrayaḥ |
Even so, when he depends on non-good persons
sbh1.3:64.25
yato na labhate kālena kālamānulomikīmavavādānuśāsanīṁ |
from whom he does not, from time to time, gain conforming admonition and instruction;
sbh1.3:64.26
kudeśe vā vasati |
or when he dwells in a bad region —
sbh1.3:64.27
yatrāsya vāsa saṁ kalpayato divā vāgraviloko vogro raverāloko bhavati |
where, when he tries to settle in to dwell, by day there is a fierce, abundant sun-glare,
sbh1.3:64.28
prabhūtaḥ, rātrau vā |
and by night
sbh1.3:64.29
uccaśabdo, mahāśabdomahājanakāyasya nirghoṣaḥ |
loud noise, the clamor of a great crowd;
sbh1.3:64.30
tīvrakaṭukaśca vātātapasaṁsparśo, manuṣyādapi bhayamayamevaṁbhāgīyo bahirdhāpāyā rdhopādāyā ntarāyo veditavyaḥ |
or sharp contact of wind and sun, or even fear of humans — such are the outward obstacles, to be understood.
sbh1.3:64.31
ayaṁ tāvadvistaravibhāgasyā gaḥ |
Such, then, is the detailed division.
sbh1.3:65.1
samāsārthaḥ punaḥ katamaḥ |
What is the summary meaning?
sbh1.3:65.2
samāsatastrividho a ntarāyaḥ |
In brief, obstacle is threefold:
The threefold division of obstacle is structurally important: prayoga-antarāya obstructs the application itself (illness, lack of basic requisites); prāvivekya-antarāya obstructs the seclusion in which application happens (busy-ness, sociability, distraction); pratisaṁlayana-antarāya obstructs retreat — the inner work of śamatha and vipaśyanā — through heedlessness, premature settling, self-aggrandizement, or fickleness. The categories cascade from gross-and-environmental to subtle-and-internal.
sbh1.3:65.3
prayogāntarāya ḥ , prāvivekyāntarāya ḥ |
the obstacle of application (prayoga), the obstacle of seclusion (prāvivekya),
sbh1.3:65.4
pratisaṁlayanā ntarāyaśca |
and the obstacle of retreat (pratisaṁlayana).
sbh1.3:65.5
tatra prayogāntarāyo yenāntarāyeṇa samava hitena saṁmukhībhūtenāśakto bhavatyapratibalaḥ sarveṇa sarvaṁ kuśalapakṣaprayoge |
Of these, the obstacle of application: that obstacle which, when present and manifest, makes him incapable and unfit — completely, entirely — for any application to the wholesome side.
sbh1.3:65.6
sa punaḥ katamaḥ |
And what is that?
sbh1.3:65.7
yadābādhako bhavati, bāḍhaglānaḥ, abhīkṣṇamasya vāto vā kupyate, pittamvā, śleṣmamvā ṣma vā , viṣūcikā vāsya kāye santiṣṭhate |
When he becomes afflicted, gravely ill — his wind, bile, phlegm, or cholera frequently rising and persisting in his body;
sbh1.3:65.8
api tvasya daśati vṛściko vā, śatapadī vā, manuṣyo vainaṁ viheṭhayatyamanuṣyo vā |
or when a scorpion bites him, or a centipede, or a human harasses him, or a non-human;
sbh1.3:65.9
na ca lābhī bhavati cīvarapiṇḍapātaśayanāsanaglānapratyayabhaiṣajyapariṣkārāṇāmayamevaṁbhāgīyaḥ prayogāntarāyo veditavyaḥ |
or when he is not a gainer of robes, alms-food, lodging, medicine, and provisions — such is the obstacle of application, to be understood.
sbh1.3:65.10
prāvivekyāntarāyaḥ katamaḥ |
What is the obstacle of seclusion?
sbh1.3:65.11
yadbhojanaguruko bhavati, bahvartho, bahukṛtyo, bahukaraṇīyaḥ, karmārāmaratiṁ rato bhavati |
When he becomes [over-]heavy in food, of much business, much to do, much to attend to; when he becomes fond of work and delights in it,
sbh1.3:65.12
teṣu teṣvitikaraṇīyeṣu prasṛtamānasaḥ |
his mind bent on those various duties;
sbh1.3:65.13
bhāṣyārāmo bhavati |
when he becomes fond of speech,
sbh1.3:65.14
śaktaḥ pratibalaḥ san |
and though capable and fit
sbh1.3:65.15
prāvivekyaprahāṇe, pratisaṁlayane, bhāvanāyāmuddeśamvādhyāyamātrakena ṇa saṁtuṣṭo nidrārāmo bhavati |
for seclusion, abandonment, retreat, and cultivation, he is content with mere recitation and study, fond of sleep,
sbh1.3:65.16
styānamiddhaparyavasthitaḥ, kusīdajātīyo nidrāsukhaṁ, pārśvasukhaṁ, śayanasukhaṁ ca svīkaroti |
seized by sloth-and-torpor, of lazy disposition, cherishing the pleasure of sleep, the pleasure of [lying on the] side, the pleasure of reclining;
sbh1.3:65.17
saṁgaṇikārāmo bhavati sārdhaṁ gṛhasthapravrajitaiḥ |
when he becomes fond of company with householders and renouncers,
sbh1.3:65.18
rājakathāmvā karoti |
engaging in king-talk,
sbh1.3:65.19
corakathāṁ vā, annakathāṁ vā, pānakathāṁ vā, vastukathāṁ vā, veśyākathāṁ vā, vīthīkathāmvā janapadamahāmātrākhyānakathāmvā, samudrākhyānakathāmvā |
or thief-talk, food-talk, drink-talk, gossip about possessions, courtesans, streets, the countryside, tales about great ministers, sea-stories —
sbh1.3:65.20
ityevaṁ bhāgīyayā anarthopasaṁhitayā kathayā kālamatināmayati |
passing the time with such useless talk,
sbh1.3:65.21
tatra cābhira ma to bhavatyabhīkṣṇeṇa na gaṇasannipātabahulo bhavati |
delighting in it, frequently in large gatherings,
sbh1.3:65.22
teṣu teṣvadhikaraṇeṣu vyākṣiptamānaso bhavati |
his mind scattered over those various matters,
sbh1.3:65.23
vyākulamānasaḥ |
distracted;
sbh1.3:65.24
saṁsargārāmo bhavati |
when he becomes fond of association,
sbh1.3:65.25
gṛhasthapravrajitānāmasamavahitānāṁ ca viyogaṁ gacchati |
and when separated from those householders and renouncers he undergoes separation-grief;
sbh1.3:65.26
prapaṁcārāmo bhavati |
when he becomes fond of conceptual proliferation,
sbh1.3:65.27
prapaṁcarataḥ avakramaṇīyeṣu pūrvaṁgamaḥ, prāvivekyeṣu nikṣiptadhuraḥ |
delighting in proliferation — taking the lead in occasions for backsliding, but in matters of seclusion one who has laid down the burden —
sbh1.3:65.28
idamevaṁbhāgīyo ima evaṁ bhāgīyā dharmāḥ pravivekāntarāyo veditavyaḥ |
such are the dharmas to be understood as the obstacle of seclusion:
sbh1.3:65.29
yaiḥ samavahitaiḥ saṁmukhībhūtairna sukaraṁ bhavatyaraṇyavanaprasthāni prāntāni śayanāsanānyadhyāvasituṁ |
[dharmas] by which, when present and manifest, it is not easy to dwell in remote lodgings in forests and woods,
sbh1.3:65.30
araṇyāni vā vṛkṣamūlāni vā śūnyāgārāṇi vā |
or at the foot of trees, or in empty huts.
sbh1.3:65.31
pratisaṁlayanāntarāyaḥ katamaḥ |
What is the obstacle of retreat?
sbh1.3:65.32
tadyathā pratisaṁlayanamucyate śamatho vipaśyanā ca |
‘Retreat’ here means śamatha (calm) and vipaśyanā (insight).
sbh1.3:65.33
tatrāsti śamathāntarāyaḥ |
There is the obstacle of calm
sbh1.3:65.34
asti vipaśyanāntarāyaḥ |
and the obstacle of insight.
sbh1.3:65.35
tatra śamathāntarāyaḥ pra bhinnaḥ adeśavāsaśca |
Of these, the obstacle of calm [comes from two things:] heedlessness and dwelling in a bad place.
sbh1.3:65.36
yathāsya pramattasya styānamiddhaṁ vā cittaṁ paryavanahati |
When, in the heedless, sloth-and-torpor wraps the mind round,
sbh1.3:65.37
śamathamātramvā āsvādayati |
or he savors mere [premature] calm,
sbh1.3:65.38
līnatvāya vā cittamupanāmayati |
or inclines the mind toward slackness,
sbh1.3:65.39
andhakārāyitatvaṁ vā cetaso bhavati |
or there is a darkened state of mind —
sbh1.3:65.40
yadrūpeṇa cādeśavāsena |
and as for [obstacles arising] from dwelling in a bad place,
sbh1.3:65.41
manuṣyakṛto vā parataḥ saṁghaṭṭo bhavati |
by some human conflict from outside [interfering].
sbh1.3:65.42
yenāsya vipaśyanāntarāyaḥ katamaḥ |
And what is the obstacle of insight?
sbh1.3:65.43
yadutātmasaṁgrāhaścāpalyañca |
Namely, self-aggrandizement and fickleness.
sbh1.3:65.44
tatrātmasaṁgraho yathāpi tadahamasmyuccakulaḥ pravrajitaḥ |
Of these, self-aggrandizement: when [he thinks] ‘I am of high family, gone forth,
sbh1.3:65.45
alīnaḥ |
undejected —
sbh1.3:65.46
anye ca na tathetyātmānamutkarṣayati |
and others are not so,’ he exalts himself,
sbh1.3:65.47
saṁgṛhṇāti |
takes himself up,
sbh1.3:65.48
parāṁśca pansa paṁsa yati |
and deprecates others.
sbh1.3:65.49
evamāḍhyakulapravrajitaḥ, adīnaḥ, evamabhirūpo darśanīyaḥ, prāsādika, evaṁ bahuśrutaḥ, sannicayaḥ |
Likewise [thinking], ‘I am gone forth from a wealthy family, undejected; I am handsome, well-favored, pleasing; I am much-learned, of much accumulation;
sbh1.3:65.50
evamahamasmin kalyāṇavākyo vākkaraṇenopetaḥ, anye ca bhikṣavo na tatheti |
I am of fair speech, endowed with the faculty of speech — and other monks are not so’ —
sbh1.3:65.51
ātmānamutkarṣayati |
he exalts himself,
sbh1.3:65.52
saṁpragṛhṇāti |
takes himself up,
sbh1.3:65.53
parāṁśca pansa paṁsa yati |
and deprecates others.
sbh1.3:65.54
sa ātmānaṁ saṁpragṛhṇātyete bhavanti |
Holding himself up [thus, he thinks of others:] ‘these
sbh1.3:65.55
bhikṣavaḥ, sthavirā, ratijñā, abhyavatīrṇabrahmacaryāḥ, tānna kālena kālaṁ paripṛcchati |
monks, elders, experienced ones, ones who have entered deeply into the holy life’ — and so he does not, from time to time, inquire of them,
sbh1.3:65.56
paripraśnīkaroti |
does not question them.
sbh1.3:65.57
te cāsya na kālena kālamavivṛtāni ca sthānāni vivṛṇvanti |
And they, in turn, do not from time to time open up to him the unopened points,
sbh1.3:65.58
vivṛtāni ca sthānāni vivṛṇvanti |
do not clarify the points already opened,
sbh1.3:65.59
na ca gambhīramarthapadaṁ |
do not — by wisdom, well and rightly — penetrate and proclaim
sbh1.3:65.60
sādhu ca suṣṭhu ca prajñayā pratividhya saṁprakāśayanti yāvadeva jñānadarśanasya viśuddhaye |
the deep meaning-words to him, [which would lead] up through the purification of knowledge-and-vision.
sbh1.3:65.61
evamasya sa ātmasaṁgrahaḥ |
Thus that self-aggrandizement of his
sbh1.3:65.62
āntarāyiko bhavati |
becomes an obstacle —
sbh1.3:65.63
yaduta vipaśyanāyāḥ |
namely, an obstacle to insight.
sbh1.3:65.64
punaraparamalpamātrakasyāvaramātrakasya jñānadarśanamātrakasya sparśavihāramātrakasya lābhī bhavati |
Further, when he gains only a slight, scant, mere knowledge-and-vision, a mere ease-of-contact dwelling,
sbh1.3:65.65
sa tena jñānamātrakeṇa |
and by that mere knowledge,
sbh1.3:65.66
darśanamātrakeṇātmānamutkarṣayati |
that mere vision, he exalts himself,
sbh1.3:65.67
saṁpragṛhṇāti |
takes himself up,
sbh1.3:65.68
sa ātmānaṁ saṁpragṛhṇaṁstāvatā saṁtuṣṭo bhavati nottari vyāyacchate |
and being thus self-aggrandizing, is content with that much and does not strive further —
sbh1.3:65.69
evamasyāntarāyaḥ kṛto bhavati |
thus too an obstacle is made for him
sbh1.3:65.70
ātmasaṁpragraheṇa yaduta vipaśyanāyāḥ nayā |
through self-aggrandizement: namely, an obstacle to insight.
sbh1.3:65.71
capalo vā punaḥ anupaśāntendriyo bhavati |
Or, again, fickle — of unpeaceful faculties,
sbh1.3:65.72
uddhatendriyaḥ, unnatendriyaḥ |
restless faculties, arrogant faculties;
sbh1.3:65.73
sa dūṣitacittī bhavati |
corrupted in mind,
sbh1.3:65.74
subhāṣitabhāṣī, duṣkṛtakarmakārī, na sthiraṁ dharmāṁścintayati |
speaking what is well-spoken yet doing what is ill-done — he does not firmly reflect on the dharmas,
sbh1.3:65.75
na dṛḍhaṁ cintayati |
does not reflect resolutely.
sbh1.3:65.76
yena vipaśyanāṁ na pūrayati na viśodhayatyevamasya cāpalyaṁ |
By this he does not bring insight to fullness, does not purify it — thus fickleness too
sbh1.3:65.77
antarāyo bhavati |
becomes his obstacle,
sbh1.3:65.78
yaduta vipaśyanāyā iti |
namely an obstacle to insight.
sbh1.3:65.79
dvau dharmau śamathāntarāyau |
[In sum:] two dharmas are obstacles to calm —
sbh1.3:65.80
yaduta pramādaḥ, ādeśavāsaśca |
namely, heedlessness and dwelling in a bad place.
sbh1.3:65.81
dvau dharmau vipaśyanāntarāyau yadutātmasaṁpragrahaścāpalyañca |
Two dharmas are obstacles to insight — namely, self-aggrandizement and fickleness.
sbh1.3:65.82
iti yaśca śamathāntarāyaḥ |
Whatever is the obstacle to calm,
sbh1.3:65.83
yaśca vipaśyanāntarāyaḥ |
and whatever is the obstacle to insight —
sbh1.3:65.84
ayamucyate pratisaṁlayanāntarāyaḥ |
this together is called the obstacle to retreat.
sbh1.3:65.85
ayaṁ ca punarantarāyasya samāsārtha iti |
And this is the summary meaning of obstacle.
sbh1.3:65.86
yaścāyaṁ samāsārthaḥ |
This summary meaning
sbh1.3:65.87
yaśca pūrvako vistaravibhāgaḥ |
together with the previous detailed division —
sbh1.3:65.88
tadekatyamabhisaṁkṣipyāntarāya ityucyate |
gathered into one is called obstacle.
sbh1.3:65.89
asya cāntarāyasya viparyayeṇā na ntarāyo veditavya iti |
And by the reverse of this obstacle, non-obstacle is to be understood:
sbh1.3:65.90
ya eṣāmantarā yā ṇāmabhāvo vigamaḥ asaṁgatirasamavadhāra cara ṇa mayamucyate ana ntarāyaḥ |
the absence, departure, non-presence, and non-occurrence of these obstacles is what is called non-obstacle.
sbh1.3:66.1
tyāgaḥ katamaḥ |
What is generosity?
sbh1.3:66.2
yaddānamanavadyaṁ cittālaṁkārārthaṁ, cittapariṣkārārthaṁ, yogasaṁbhārārthaṁ, uttamārthasya prāptaye dadāti |
That blameless gift which one gives for the sake of adorning the mind, for the sake of refining the mind, for the sake of the requisites of yoga, and for the attainment of the highest good.
sbh1.3:66.3
tatra ko dadāti kasmai dadāti kutra dadāti kena ca dadāti |
Here [the structure of the analysis is]: who gives, to whom does one give, what does one give, by what does one give,
The six-question framework — who, to whom, what, by what, how, why — is the standard Yogācāra analytical schema applied to any practice (also visible in the kena/kiṁ/kutaḥ/kathaṁ framing of indriya-saṁvara at 35.16–18). Sections 65–70 answer each question in turn: 65 = giver-and-recipients; 67–69 = the thing given (sentient and non-sentient); 70 = means, manner, motive.
sbh1.3:66.4
kathaṁ dadāti |
how does one give,
sbh1.3:66.5
kasmāddadāti |
why does one give —
sbh1.3:66.6
yenāsya dānama na vadyaṁ bhavati |
by [answering all of] which, his giving becomes blameless.
sbh1.3:67.1
āha |
Answer:
sbh1.3:67.2
dātā, dānapatirdadāti |
the giver — the patron — gives.
sbh1.3:67.3
ayamucyate dātā |
A giver (dātṛ) is the one whose own thing is given;
dātṛ (giver) and dāna-pati (patron) are distinguished: anyone whose own thing is given is a giver; the patron is specifically the one who gives willingly. The distinction matters because one can be a giver under coercion (e.g., taxation, theft) without being a patron; only the willing giver is a patron, and only the willing giving constitutes the tyāga requisite.
sbh1.3:67.4
yasya svakaṁ dīyate |
when that [thing] belongs to one who wants to give,
sbh1.3:67.5
tacca dātukāmasya, nādātukāmasya, ayamucyate dānapatiḥ |
not to one who does not want to give, he is called a patron (dāna-pati).
sbh1.3:67.6
tatra kutra dadāti |
And to whom does one give?
sbh1.3:67.7
āha |
Answer:
sbh1.3:67.8
caturṣu dadāti |
one gives to four [kinds of recipient]:
sbh1.3:67.9
duḥkhitāyoga pa kāriṇe |
the suffering, the helpful,
sbh1.3:67.10
iṣṭāya, viśiṣṭāya ca |
the dear, and the distinguished.
sbh1.3:67.11
tatra duḥkhitāyeti |
Of these, ‘the suffering’ means:
sbh1.3:67.12
kṛpaṇā vā, adhvagā vā, yācanakā vā, andhā vā, badhirā vā, anāthā vā, apratisaraṇā vā, upakaraṇavikalā iti |
the pitiable, travelers, beggars, the blind, the deaf, the helpless, those without recourse, those lacking life-requisites —
sbh1.3:67.13
ye vā punaranye a pyevaṁbhāgīyā ḥ |
and others of such kind.
sbh1.3:67.14
upakāriṇaḥ katame tadyathā mātāpitaraṁ rau āpāyakapoṣakaṁ kau , samvardhakami kāvi ti |
Helpers are: mother and father, those who nurture and rear one,
sbh1.3:67.15
ye vā punaraṭavīkāntārāduttārayanti, durbhikṣādvā, paracakrabhayādvā, bandhanādvā |
or those who deliver one from a forest-wilderness, from famine, from fear of enemy armies, from bondage,
sbh1.3:67.16
ābādhakādvā hitopadeśakāścāsya bhavanti |
or from sickness; and those who counsel one's welfare,
sbh1.3:67.17
sukhopadeśakā, hitasukhopasaṁhārakā, utpannotpanneṣu cādhikaraṇeṣu sahāyakāḥ, sahanandinaḥ, sahaśokā, āpatsu caivaṁ na parityajanti iti ye vā punaranye a pyevaṁbhāgīyā ima upakāriṇaḥ |
counsel one's ease, bring about welfare and ease, help in matters that have arisen, share one's joys, share one's sorrows, and do not abandon one in danger — these and others of such kind are the helpers.
sbh1.3:67.18
iṣṭāḥ katame |
Who are the dear?
sbh1.3:67.19
ye saṁstutā ḥ |
Acquaintances
sbh1.3:67.20
yeṣāmasyāntike bhavati |
toward whom one feels
sbh1.3:67.21
prema vā, gauravamvā, bhaktivādo vā, ālaptakāḥ, saṁstutakāḥ sapriyakāśca bhavanti |
love, respect, or devoted speech — those one greets, knows well, holds beloved —
sbh1.3:67.22
iti vā punaranye a pyevaṁbhāgīyā ima ucyate iṣṭāḥ |
and others of such kind. These are called the dear.
sbh1.3:68.1
viśiṣṭāḥ katame |
Who are the distinguished?
sbh1.3:68.2
te śrava ma ṇabrāhmaṇā ḥ sādhurūpasammatāḥ |
Recluses and brahmins reckoned as good —
sbh1.3:68.3
avyābādhyāḥ avyābādhyaratāḥ, vigatarāgāḥ, rāgavinayāya pratipannāḥ, vigatadveṣā dveṣavinayāya pratipannāḥ |
the non-harming, those who delight in non-harming; the lust-free and those practicing for the subduing of lust; the hatred-free and those practicing for the subduing of hatred;
sbh1.3:68.4
vigatamohā, mohavinayāya pratipannā iti |
the delusion-free and those practicing for the subduing of delusion.
sbh1.3:68.5
ye vā punaranye a pyevaṁ bhāgīyā ima ucyante viśiṣṭāḥ |
These and others of such kind are called the distinguished.
sbh1.3:69.1
tatra kiṁ dadātītyāha |
What, then, does one give?
sbh1.3:69.2
samāsataḥ sattvasaṁkhyātamasatva ttva saṁkhyātaṁ ca |
In brief, one gives a thing reckoned as either sentient or non-sentient.
sbh1.3:69.3
vastu dadāti |
One gives the [appropriate] thing.
sbh1.3:69.4
tatra sattvasaṁkhyātamvastu katamat |
What is the sentient-reckoned thing?
sbh1.3:69.5
tadyathā putradānaṁ dāsīdāsakarmakarapau ruṣe yahastyaśvagaveḍakakukkuṭastrīpuruṣadārakadārikamiti |
Namely the giving of son, female slave, male slave, worker, attendant, elephant, horse, cow, sheep, fowl, woman, man, boy, or girl —
sbh1.3:69.6
yadvā punaranyadapyevaṁ bhāgīyaṁ vastu |
or other such things.
sbh1.3:69.7
adhyātmamvā punarupādāya karacaraṇaśiromānsa māṁsa rudhiravasādīnyanuprayacchati |
Or with respect to one's own body — hand, foot, head, flesh, blood, fat, and so on — he offers these.
sbh1.3:69.8
idamapi puruṣasaṁkhyātaṁ dānaṁ yatra bodhisattvabodhāḥ saṁdṛśyante |
This [self-giving] too is a sentient-reckoned gift, [the kind] in which bodhisattvas are seen [to engage];
Self-giving (offering parts of one's own body — head, hand, flesh, blood) is here explicitly bracketed off as a bodhisattva practice that is not the tyāga requisite intended in the śrāvakabhūmi context. This is one of the few places where the source explicitly demarcates between śrāvaka and bodhisattva paths within a discussion that could apply to either.
sbh1.3:69.9
asmiṁtvarthe nedaṁ dānamabhipretaṁ |
but it is not the gift intended in the present context.
sbh1.3:69.10
yeṣu tu sarveṣvasyaiśvaryaṁ bhavati |
[Here intended is rather:] all those [sentient beings] over whom he has lordship,
sbh1.3:69.11
vaśitā ca prabhaviṣṇutā ca |
mastery, and effective power,
sbh1.3:69.12
arhati ca tānsatvā ttvā n pareṣāṁ pratipādayituṁ |
and whom he is fit to bestow on others.
sbh1.3:69.13
pratipādayaṁścātmānamanavadyaṁ karoti |
Bestowing them, he makes himself blameless:
sbh1.3:69.14
na taddhetostatpratyayaṁ pare manānsi nāṁsi pradūṣayanti |
[the gift is given in such a way that] others do not corrupt their minds on its account, by way of its condition;
sbh1.3:69.15
ye ca satvā ttvāḥ pareṣu pratipāditā ḥ stena vyāpāditā bhavantīdamucyate anavadyaṁ satva ttva vastudānaṁ |
and those beings bestowed on others are not afflicted by them — this is called the blameless gift of sentient-things.
sbh1.3:70.1
asattvasaṁkhyātamvastu katamat |
What is the non-sentient-reckoned thing?
sbh1.3:70.2
tadyathā dhanavastu, dhānyavastu |
Namely: wealth-things, grain-things,
sbh1.3:70.3
deśa vastu |
and land-things.
sbh1.3:70.4
tatra dhanavastu tadyathā maṇimuktāvaiḍūryaśaṁkhaśilāpravāḍā lā śmagarttapusāragaṇvajātarūparajatalohitikādakṣiṇāvartamit i |
Of these, wealth-things: gems, pearls, beryl, conch-shell, stone, coral, agate, gold, silver, copper, right-spiraling [shells];
sbh1.3:70.5
yadvā punaranyadapyevaṁ bhāgīyaṁ ratnamvā, hiraṇyamvā, rūpyamvā, vastramvā, bhāṇḍopaskaraṁ vā, gandhajātaṁ vā, mālyajātamvā, idamucyate dhanaṁ |
or any other such jewel, gold, silver, cloth, vessel or furniture, fragrance, or garland — this is called wealth.
sbh1.3:70.6
dhānyaṁ katamat |
What is grain?
sbh1.3:70.7
yatkiṁñcid bhojyaṁ vā, peyamvā, tadyathā yavā vā, śālirvā, godhūmā vā, kolā vā, kulatthā vā, tilā vā, māṣā vā, ikṣurasamvā so vā , mṛdvīkārasamvā so vā , iti yadvā punarapyevaṁbhāgīyamidamucyate dhānyaṁ |
Whatever is food or drink: barley, rice, wheat, jujube, horse-gram, sesame, beans, sugar-cane juice, grape juice — or any other such thing. This is called grain.
sbh1.3:71.1
deśavastu katamat |
What is the land-thing?
sbh1.3:71.2
tadyathā kṣetravastu gṛhavastvāpaṇavastu, puṇyaśālāvihārapratiṣṭhāpanamiti |
Namely: a field, a house, a shop, the establishment of a merit-hall or a monastery —
sbh1.3:71.3
yadvā punaranyadapyevaṁbhāgīyamidamucyate deśavastu |
or any other such thing. This is called land-thing.
sbh1.3:71.4
tatra yacca satva sattva saṁkhyātamvastu idaṁ dadātīti |
[All of these] — the sentient-reckoned thing and the [non-sentient-reckoned thing] — these are what one gives.
sbh1.3:72.1
kena dadatīti |
By what does one give?
sbh1.3:72.2
yā ca alobhasahagatā cetanā, cittābhisaṁskāro manaskarma |
By volition accompanied by non-greed — that is, mental conditioning, mental action —
sbh1.3:72.3
yacca tatsamutthāpitaṁ kāyakarma |
and by the bodily action produced therefrom,
sbh1.3:72.4
vākkarma de śa vastuparityāgāya, svasaṁtāne vā |
and the verbal action, for the relinquishment of [whatever] thing, [whether external] or in one's own continuum:
sbh1.3:72.5
anena dadāti |
by these one gives.
sbh1.3:72.6
tatra kathaṁ dadātīti |
How does one give?
sbh1.3:72.7
śraddhayā dadāti |
One gives with faith;
sbh1.3:72.8
āgamadṛṣṭiḥ, phaladarśī, satkṛtya dadāti |
with scriptural tradition as one's view, seeing the fruit, one gives respectfully;
sbh1.3:72.9
praṇatacittaḥ svahastaṁ hastābhyāṁ dadāti |
with humble mind, with one's own hand, with both hands;
sbh1.3:72.10
anapaviddha ḥ kālena dadāti |
without contempt, at the right time;
sbh1.3:72.11
yadaitatpareṣāmupayogyaṁ syātparānanupahatya dadāti |
in such a way that the gift will be of use to others, without injuring [those others] in the giving;
sbh1.3:72.12
dharmeṇa, samayena, asāhasena, samudānayitvā samudānīya śuci dadāti |
by dharma, by proper means, without violence — having accumulated [the gift] rightly, one gives what is pure,
sbh1.3:72.13
praṇītaṁ kalpikaṁ dadāti |
what is fine, what is allowable;
sbh1.3:72.14
yena na pare sāvadyā bhavanti |
[gives in such a way] that others are not made blameworthy [by accepting it],
sbh1.3:72.15
nātmā |
nor is oneself [made blameworthy by giving it];
sbh1.3:72.16
abhīkṣṇaṁ dadāti |
one gives frequently;
sbh1.3:72.17
vinīya sātma yanmalaṁ sannidhimalaṁ ca dānaṁ dadāti |
having disciplined the stain of stinginess and the stain of hoarding, one gives the gift;
sbh1.3:72.18
pūrvameva dānātsumanā dadacci daṁści ttaṁ prasādayati |
with glad mind before the giving, with mind clarified during the giving,
sbh1.3:72.19
datvā vā vipratisārī bhavati |
and one is not remorseful afterward.
sbh1.3:72.20
evaṁ dadāti |
Thus one gives.
sbh1.3:72.21
kasmāddadāti |
Why does one give?
sbh1.3:72.22
āha |
Answer:
sbh1.3:72.23
kāruṇyādvā dadāti |
one gives out of compassion —
sbh1.3:72.24
yaduta duḥkhiteṣu kṛtajñatāyā tayā dadāti |
namely, to the suffering; out of gratitude —
sbh1.3:72.25
yaduta upakāriṣu premṇā, gauraveṇa, bhaktyā dadāti |
namely, to the helpers; with love, respect, and devotion —
sbh1.3:72.26
yaduta iṣṭeṣu laukikalokāntaraviśeṣaprārthanayā dadāti |
namely, to the dear; with the prayer for worldly and otherworldly distinction —
sbh1.3:72.27
yaduta viśiṣṭeṣu |
namely, to the distinguished.
sbh1.3:72.28
tasmāt |
Therefore
sbh1.3:72.29
dadātītyucyate |
[in this way alone] is it said that ‘one gives.’
sbh1.3:72.30
ebhirākārairato ’sya gṛhiṇo vā, pravrajitasya vā cittālaṁkārārthaṁ, cittapariṣkārārthaṁ |
By these modes — for him, whether householder or renouncer — for the sake of adorning the mind, for the sake of refining the mind,
sbh1.3:72.31
yogasaṁbhārārthamuttamārthasya prāptaye taddānamanavadyaṁ bhavati |
for the sake of the requisites of yoga, for the attainment of the highest good, that gift becomes blameless.
sbh1.3:72.32
ayamucyate tyāgaḥ |
This is called generosity.

sbh1.3:74.1

with energy, enthusiastic, of firm valor,

sbh1.3:74.29

sbh1.3:74.38

sbh1.3:74.39
itaretareṣu cīvarapiṇḍapātena, śayanāsanena tuṣṭo bhavati, saṁtuṣṭaḥ, sa labdhvā cīvaraṁ lūhaṁ vā, praṇītamvā notkaṇṭhati |
With whatever robes, alms-food, [or] lodging [he obtains], he is satisfied, content; whether obtaining inferior or fine robes, he does not long [for more],

[and] does not become anxious;
sbh1.3:74.41

unclinging, [as elaborated] above.

sbh1.3:74.44

not others — such as children or [household] men,

sbh1.3:74.52
kathaṁ dhutaguṇasamanvāgato bhavati |
How is he endowed with ascetic qualities?
sbh1.3:74.53
piṇḍapātiko bhavati |
He is an alms-food taker [piṇḍapātika],
The ascetic-quality list now brackets local forms rather than importing parallel/normalized labels: piṇḍapātika, sāvadāna-piṇḍapātika, ekāsanika, and naiṣadyika. The sequential alms-food taker corresponds functionally to the better-known sāpadāna-cārin, but the bracket follows the local line.
sbh1.3:74.54
sāvadānapiṇḍapātikaḥ |
a sequential alms-food taker [sāvadāna-piṇḍapātika],
sbh1.3:74.55
ekāsanikaḥ, khalu paścādbhaktikaḥ |
a one-seat eater [ekāsanika], an after-meal-only refuser [khalupaścādbhaktika],
sbh1.3:74.56
traicīvariko, nāmatikaḥ, pānsu pāṁsu kūlikaḥ |
a three-robe wearer [traicīvarika], a felt-wearer [nāmatika], a refuse-rag wearer [pāṁsukūlika],
sbh1.3:74.57
āraṇyako, vṛkṣamūlikaḥ ābhyavakāśikaḥ |
a forest-dweller [āraṇyaka], a tree-root dweller [vṛkṣamūlika], an open-air dweller [ābhyavakāśika],
sbh1.3:74.58
śmāśāniko, naiṣadyikaḥ |
a cemetery dweller [śmāśānika], a sitter [naiṣadyika, one who does not lie down],
sbh1.3:74.59
yāthāsaṁstarikaḥ |
an any-bed acceptor [yāthāsaṁstarika].
sbh1.3:74.60
ta ete piṇḍapātacīvaraśayanāsanamārabhya dvādaśakā dhuta guṇā bhavanti |
These [are] the twelve ascetic qualities, with reference to alms-food, robes, [and] lodging —
The twelve (or thirteen) dhutaguṇa — paiṇḍapātika, sāpadāna-cārin, aikāsanika, khalupaścādbhaktika, traicīvarika, nāmatika, pāṁsukūlika, āraṇyaka, vṛkṣamūlika, ābhyavakāśika, śmāśānika, naiṣadika, yāthāsaṁstarika — match the Pāli dhutaṅga list of the Visuddhimagga II, with terminology and ordering essentially identical. The Yogācāra count of thirteen treats piṇḍapāta and sāpadāna-cāra as distinct; the count of twelve subsumes the second under the first. The functional analysis at 80.1–16 maps each dhutaguṇa onto the specific kind of lust (rāga) it counteracts — a Yogācāra abhidharma move that converts the ascetic list from a catalogue of practices into a typology of antidotes.
sbh1.3:74.61
trayodaśā śa vā |
or [counted as] thirteen.
sbh1.3:74.62
tatra piṇḍapātikatvaṁ bhidyamānaṁ dvidhā bhavati |
[Counted as thirteen,] the alms-food-taker quality, when divided, becomes twofold:
sbh1.3:74.63
prāptapiṇḍapātikaśca, sāvadānapiṇḍapātika ta yā veśyānuveśyakulāni bhikṣitvā, paryaṭitvā paryaṭya , yathālabdhaṁ yathopasampannaṁ piṇḍapātaṁ paribhuṁkte, no tu uccaiḥ śuṇḍāṁ praṇidhāya kulāni upasaṁkrāmati |
[Damaged/Conjectural]: the [plain] alms-food-taker [who eats] as obtained, and the sequential alms-food-taker who, having begged from house to house, from family to family, and having completed the round, enjoys the alms-food as obtained, as suitable — not approaching families with raised expectation [thinking]:
The printed/extracted veśyānuveśya-kulāni is treated as corrupt or noisy in the sequential alms-food context. The practice being defined is the house-to-house alms round (sāvadāna/sāpadāna), not approach to prostitute families; English therefore marks the line conjectural/corrupt and renders the functional sense 'from house to house, from family to family.'
sbh1.3:74.64
ato a haṁ lapsye, praṇītaṁ khādanīyaṁ, bhojanīyaṁ, yāvadāptaṁ |
‘There I shall obtain fine chewable and edible food, enough to satisfy me.’
sbh1.3:74.65
tatra piṇḍapātikatvamaviśeṣeṇārabhya dvādaśa bhavanti |
When alms-food-taking is taken without distinction [as a single quality], they become twelve;
sbh1.3:74.66
prabhedaṁ punaḥ ārabhya trayodaśa ḥ |
when the [twofold] division is taken, thirteen.

idamucyate |

sbh1.3:74.72

one-seat-eating.
sbh1.3:75.1

discarded — on the road, in the lane, at the crossroads, at a junction, on the path or off-path; mixed with feces, mixed with urine, [or] mingled with feces, urine, pus, blood, [or] mucus —
sbh1.3:77.8

from which he removes what is impure, [and,] taking the [usable] substance, cleansing [it], sewing [it], dyeing [it], wears [it] — thus he is a refuse-rag wearer.

sbh1.3:78.1

sbh1.3:78.5
kathamābhyavakāśiko bhavati |
How does he become an ābhyavakāśika (open-sky dweller)?
Restored from PDF 2313-2314. The pre-existing JSON had a stitched clause evaṁ vṛkṣamūliko kathaṁ śmāśāniko bhavati collapsing the end of the vṛkṣamūlika definition with the opening of the śmāśānika definition, dropping the entire ābhyavakāśika (open-sky dweller) definition between them. The dhutaguṇa enumeration of dwelling-places now reads in source order: vṛkṣamūlika → ābhyavakāśika → śmāśānika.

abhyavakāśe vāsaṁ kalpayati |

He makes his abode in a cemetery, [the place] where the dead are
sbh1.3:78.11
abhinirhriyata evaṁ śmāśāniko bhavati |
carried out — thus he is a cemetery dweller.
sbh1.3:79.1
kathaṁ naiṣadyiko bhavati |
How is he a sitter [naiṣadyika]?
sbh1.3:79.2
maṁce vā, pīṭhe vā, tṛṇasaṁstare vā niṣadyayā kālamatināmayati |
On couch, stool, [or] grass-mat, he passes the time [only] by sitting,
sbh1.3:79.3
no tu maṁcā ma caṁ vā, pīṭhaṁ vā, kuḍyamvā, vṛkṣamūlaṁ vṛkṣamūlamvā, tṛṇasaṁstaramvā,parṇasaṁstaramvā, niśritya pṛṣṭha vā pṛṣṭhaṁ vā , pārśvamvā dadātyevaṁ naiṣadyiko bhavati |
not leaning his back or side against a couch, stool, wall, tree-root, grass-mat, or leaf-mat — thus he is a sitter.
sbh1.3:79.4
kathaṁ yāthāsaṁstariko bhavati |
How is he an any-bed acceptor [yāthāsaṁstarika]?
sbh1.3:79.5
yasmin tṛṇasaṁstare vā, parṇasaṁstare vā śayyāṁ kalpayati |
Wherever — on grass-mat or leaf-mat — he prepares the bed:
sbh1.3:79.6
tṛṇasaṁstaramvā, parṇasaṁstaramvā sakṛtyañcaiva saṁskṛtaṁ bhavati |
whatever grass-mat or leaf-mat has been once arranged,
sbh1.3:79.7
tathaiva śayyāṁ kalpayati |
just so he uses [it as] the bed,
sbh1.3:79.8
no tu punarvikopayati |
he does not change it,
sbh1.3:79.9
abhisaṁskaroti caivaṁ ya yā thāsaṁstariko bhavati |
he [merely] arranges [it as it is] — thus he is an any-bed acceptor.
sbh1.3:79.10
kenaite dhutaguṇā ucyante tadyathā ūrṇā vā, karpāsaṁ vā, dhutaṁ bhavatyasaṁvṛttamiti |
Why are these called ‘ascetic qualities’ [dhutaguṇa, lit. ‘shaken-off qualities’]? Namely, just as wool or cotton, when shaken [dhuta], becomes loose,
The folk-etymology dhuta-guṇa ← dhuta (shaken, shaken-off): just as wool/cotton becomes loose and workable when shaken, so the mind becomes loose and workable when 'shaken' free of attachment to food, robes, and lodging through these ascetic disciplines. The simile is also found in the Visuddhimagga and is the standard explanation across Buddhist sects for why these are called 'dhutaguṇa.'
sbh1.3:79.11
ta cca ta smin samaye mṛdu ca bhavati |
and at that time is soft,
sbh1.3:79.12
laghu ca, karmaṇyaṁ ca, yaduta sūtrābhinirhāre vā, tūlāvahade te tūlābhinirhāre vā |
light, [and] workable — namely for spinning thread or for stuffing cotton —
sbh1.3:79.13
evameva ihaikatyasya piṇḍapātarāgeṇa piṇḍapāte cittaṁ saktaṁ bhavati |
just so, for someone here, by lust for alms-food the mind is attached to alms-food,
sbh1.3:79.14
saṁsaktaṁ cīvararāgeṇa śayanāsanarā geṇa śayanāsane cittaṁ saktaṁ bhavati |
joined together [with it]; [and] by lust for robes, lust for lodging, the mind is attached,
sbh1.3:79.15
saṁsaktaṁ |
joined together [with them].
sbh1.3:79.16
sa ebhirdhutaguṇairviśodhayati ṛjūkaroti |
He, by these ascetic qualities, purifies [it], straightens [it],
sbh1.3:79.17
mṛdukarmaṇyamārjavamāsravamvidheyaṁ yaduta brahmacaryavāsāya tenocyante dhutaguṇā iti |
makes [it] soft, workable, straightforward, controllable — namely for the dwelling of the holy life; therefore they are called ‘ascetic qualities.’
sbh1.3:80.1
tatra piṇḍapātaprahīṇabhojanarāgasya prahāṇāya ekāsaniko bhavati |
Now, for the abandonment of lust for plentiful food in alms-food, he becomes a one-seat eater.
sbh1.3:80.2
prabhūtabhojanarāgasya prahāṇāya nāmatiko bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for plentiful food, he becomes an after-meal refuser.
sbh1.3:80.3
praṇītacīvararāgasya prahāṇāya nāmatiko bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for fine robes, he becomes a felt-wearer.
The ascetic-quality antidote list at 80.1–16 now avoids slash-hedges where the Sanskrit is straightforward: praṇīta-cīvara-rāga is rendered consistently as lust for fine robes, not differentiated as 'soft' versus 'many' robes without local evidence. The compound pārśva-sukha-śayana-sukha-rāga is likewise two coordinated comforts, not a side/lying alternative. At 80.11 the root has saṁsarga-rāga ('association-lust'), not the preceding saṁsparśa-rāga ('contact-lust'), matching the forest-dweller's withdrawal from social association. Similarly, varṇa at 40.20 is complexion, dhārayati at 75.11 is keeping more than three robes, and apratigupta-bhojī at 80.45 is eating openly as a sharer.
sbh1.3:80.4
praṇītacīvararāgasya prahāṇāya traicīvariko bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for fine robes, he becomes a three-robe wearer.
sbh1.3:80.5
cīvare mṛdusaṁsparśarāgasya prahāṇāya pānsu pāṁsu kūliko bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for soft-contact robes, he becomes a refuse-rag wearer.
sbh1.3:80.6
śayanāsanarāgaścaturvidhaḥ |
Lust for lodging is fourfold,
sbh1.3:80.7
tadyathā saṁsparśarāgaḥ |
namely: contact-lust [for soft surfaces],
sbh1.3:80.8
pratiśrayarāgaḥ |
shelter-lust,
sbh1.3:80.9
pārśvasukhaśayanasukharāgaḥ |
lust for side-comfort and lying-down comfort,
sbh1.3:80.10
āstaraṇapratyāstaraṇopacchādanarāgaḥ |
[and] lust for spread, coverlet, [and] covering.
sbh1.3:80.11
tatra saṁsargarāgasya prahāṇāya āraṇyako bhavati |
Now, for the abandonment of association-lust, he becomes a forest-dweller.
sbh1.3:80.12
pratiśrayarāgasya prahāṇāya vṛkṣamūlikaḥ āmyavakāśikaḥ |
For the abandonment of shelter-lust, he becomes a tree-root dweller [or] an open-air dweller,
sbh1.3:80.13
śmāśāniko bhavati |
[or] becomes a cemetery dweller.
sbh1.3:80.14
api ca śmāśānikatvaṁ middhena rāgasya prahāṇāya bhavati |
Moreover, cemetery-dwelling is [also] for the abandonment of lust [arising] through torpor.
sbh1.3:80.15
pārśvasukhaśayanāsanasukharāgasya prahāṇāya naiṣadyiko bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for side-comfort and lying-down comfort, he becomes a sitter.
sbh1.3:80.16
āstaraṇapratyāstaraṇopacchāda na rāgasya prahāṇāya yāthāsāṁstariko bhavatyevaṁ dhutaguṇa samanvāgato bhavati |
For the abandonment of lust for spread, coverlet, [and] covering, he becomes an any-bed acceptor. — Thus he is endowed with ascetic qualities.
sbh1.3:80.17
kathaṁ prāsādiko bhavati |
How is he pleasing?
sbh1.3:80.18
prāsādikenāti bhi kramapratikrameṇa samanvāgato bhavatyālokitavyalokitena saṁjimita sāṁmiṁjita prasāritena sāṁghāṭīcīvarapātradhāraṇenaivaṁ prāsādiko bhavati |
He is endowed with pleasing setting-forth-and-returning, looking ahead, looking around, bending and stretching, [and] wearing of saṅghāṭi, robes, [and] bowl — thus he is pleasing.
sbh1.3:80.19
kathaṁ mātrajño bhavati |
How is he knowing the right measure?
sbh1.3:80.20
iha śrāddhā brāhmaṇagṛhapatayo vyarthaṁ pracārayati |
When faithful brāhmaṇa-householders provide for him as one gone forth —
sbh1.3:80.21
yaduta cīvara piṇḍapāta śayanāsanaglānapratyayabhaiṣajyapariṣkāraistatra pratigra heṇa mātrāṁ jānāti |
namely with robes, alms-food, lodging, medicine, [and other] requisites — in receiving them, he knows the measure.
sbh1.3:80.22
evaṁ mātrajño bhavati |
Thus he is knowing the right measure.
sbh1.3:80.23
kathaṁ satpuruṣadharmasamanvāgato bhavati |
How is he endowed with the dharmas of noble persons?
sbh1.3:80.24
uccakulapravrajito vā sannāḍhyakulapravrajito vā, abhirūpo vā, darśanīyaḥ, prāsādiko, bahuśrutopakaraṇenopetaḥ |
Gone forth from a high family, or from a wealthy family, handsome, well-favored to see, pleasing, much-learned, endowed with abundant requisites,
sbh1.3:80.25
anyatamānyatamasya vā jñānamātrasya darśanamātrakasparśavihāra sārthakasya lābhī sannetenātmānamutkarṣayati |
[Damaged/Conjectural]: [or being] a gainer of some kind of mere knowledge, mere vision, [or] mere ease-of-contact dwelling — by these he does not exalt himself,
The extracted root reads etenātmānam utkarṣayati | parāṁś ca paṁsayati without negators, but that would make this a self-exalting, other-disparaging person precisely in the definition of being endowed with the dharmas of noble persons. The expected reading is na ... na ...; English restores the negative sense and marks the line [Damaged/Conjectural].
sbh1.3:80.26
parāṁśca pansa paṁsa yati |
nor deprecate others;
sbh1.3:80.27
dharmānudharmapratipanno bhavatyevaṁ satpuruṣadharmasamanvāgato kathaṁ paṇḍitaliṁgasamanvāgato bhavati |
he practices Dharma in accordance with Dharma — thus he is endowed with the dharmas of noble persons. — How is he endowed with the marks of the wise?
sbh1.3:80.28
karmalakṣaṇo bālaḥ, karmalakṣaṇaḥ paṇḍitaḥ |
The fool is action-marked, [and] the wise [too] is action-marked.
sbh1.3:80.29
yathākathamiti bālo duścintitacintī bhavati |
How [so]? The fool is an ill-thought-out thinker,
sbh1.3:80.30
durbhāṣitabhāṣī, duṣkṛtakarmakārī, paṇḍitaḥ punaḥ sucintitacintī bhavati |
an ill-spoken speaker, an ill-done-doer. The wise, on the other hand, is a well-thought-out thinker,
sbh1.3:80.31
subhāṣitabhāṣī, sukṛtakarmakārī |
a well-spoken speaker, a well-done-doer.
sbh1.3:80.32
evaṁ paṇḍitaliṁgasamanvāgato bhavati |
Thus he is endowed with the marks of the wise.
sbh1.3:80.33
kathaṁ kṣamo bhavati |
How is he patient?
sbh1.3:80.34
ākruṣṭo na pratyākrośati |
Abused, he does not abuse in return;
sbh1.3:80.35
roṣito na pratiroṣayati |
angered [against], he does not anger in return;
sbh1.3:80.36
vādito na prativādayati |
disputed [against], he does not dispute [back];
sbh1.3:80.37
bhaṇḍito na pratibhaṇḍayati |
reviled, he does not revile in return.
sbh1.3:80.38
sa cāyuṣmān kṣamo bhavati |
He, [in such cases,] is patient.
sbh1.3:80.39
śītasyoṣṇasya jighatsāyā ḥ , pipāsāyāḥ, daṁśakamaśakavātātapasarīsṛpasaṁsparśānāṁ ṇāṁ , parato duruktānāṁ, durāgatānāṁ, vacanapathānāṁ |
Of cold, heat, hunger, thirst, gnats, mosquitoes, wind, sun, [and] creeping creatures’ contacts; of ill-spoken [words] from others, of ill-arrived [hardships], [and] of [hostile] paths of speech;
sbh1.3:80.40
śārīrikāṇāṁ vedanānāṁ, duḥkhānāṁ, tīvrāṇāṁ, kharāṇāṁ, kaṭukānāṁ, amana āpānāṁ prāṇahāriṇīnāṁ kṣamo bhavatyadhivāsanajātīyaḥ |
of bodily feelings — painful, intense, harsh, biting, disagreeable, life-threatening — he is patient, of accommodating nature.
sbh1.3:80.41
evaṁ kṣamo bhavati |
Thus he is patient.
sbh1.3:80.42
kathaṁ sū surato bhavati |
How is he gentle?
sbh1.3:80.43
yathāpi tanmaitreṇa kāya karmaṇā samanvāgato bhavati |
When [he is] endowed with friendly bodily action,
sbh1.3:80.44
maitreṇa vākkarmaṇā, maitreṇa manaskarmaṇā, śāsturantike vijñānāṁ ca, sabrahmacāriṇāṁ, sādhāraṇaparibhogī ca bhavatyaparibhogī ca |
with friendly verbal action, [and] friendly mental action — in the presence of the Teacher, the wise, [and his] co-religionists — [being] a sharer of common enjoyments and not a separate-enjoyer,
sbh1.3:80.45
bhavatyapratiguptabhojī |
he eats openly as a sharer [at meals],
sbh1.3:80.46
lābhairdhārmikairdharmapratilabdhaiḥ, pātragataiḥ, pātraparyāpannaiḥ śīlasāmānyagataśca bhavati |
with gains lawful, Dharma-obtained, [whatever is] bowl-gone or bowl-included [he shares]; [he is] sharing in virtue
sbh1.3:80.47
dṛṣṭisāmānyagataśca |
and sharing in view.
sbh1.3:80.48
sa ebhiḥ ṣaḍbhiḥ saṁrajanīyairdharmaiḥ saṁraṁjanīyairdharmaiḥ samanvāgataḥ priyakaraṇairgurukaraṇairsthavādakaraṇaiḥ sukhasaṁvāsyo bhavati |
He, endowed with these six conciliatory dharmas — making one dear, respected, [and] esteemed — becomes pleasant to live with,
The ṣaṭ-sāmīcī-dharma (six conciliatory dharmas) — friendly bodily action, friendly verbal action, friendly mental action, sharing in gains (open-eating at meals), sharing in virtue [śīla-sāmānyatā], and sharing in view [dṛṣṭi-sāmānyatā] — are the standard list of the six sāraṇīya-dhamma (Pāli) / sāmīcī-dharma (Skt.) of conciliatory living. Identical in Pāli AN 6.11–12 and across Yogācāra sources, defining the conditions of harmonious saṅgha life.
sbh1.3:80.49
aviheṭhanajātīyaḥ |
of non-harming nature,
sbh1.3:80.50
abhinandanti cainaṁ vijñāḥ |
and the wise rejoice in him,
sbh1.3:80.51
sabrahmacāriṇaḥ |
[as do his] co-religionists,
sbh1.3:80.52
ekavyavasāyaḥ |
of one resolve.
sbh1.3:80.53
evaṁ sū su rato bhavati |
Thus he is gentle.
sbh1.3:80.54
kathaṁ peśalo bhavati |
How is he lovely?
sbh1.3:80.55
vigatabhṛkuṭirbhavatyuttānamukhavarṇaḥ, smitasārddhaṁgamaḥpūrvābhibhāṣī |
Free from frowning, of open-faced complexion, accompanied by a smile, the first to greet,
sbh1.3:80.56
priyavādī |
of dear speech,
sbh1.3:80.57
saṁgrahaśīlaḥ |
of welcoming disposition [saṁgraha-śīla],
Saṁgraha-śīla here describes an approachable, welcoming disposition in the definition of being lovely (peśala). The previous bracket pūrva-saṅgāhika was not local to the line. Ātta-santāna at 78.58 is taken functionally as composed or gathered bearing, rather than the literal but opaque 'well-bound continuum.'
sbh1.3:80.58
āttasantānaḥ ḥ |
of composed bearing.
sbh1.3:80.59
evaṁ peśalo bhavati |
Thus he is lovely.
sbh1.3:80.60
sa ebhidharmaiḥ samanvāgato dharmakāmo bhavati |
He, endowed with these dharmas, is desirous of Dharma,
sbh1.3:80.61
guṇakāmaḥ |
desirous of [the] qualities,
sbh1.3:80.62
na lābhasatkārakīrtiślokakāmaḥ |
not desirous of gain, honor, fame, [or] eulogy.
sbh1.3:80.63
na sa māropikayā mithyādṛṣṭyā samanvāgato bhavati |
He is not endowed with the wrong view of superimposing [what does not exist],
sbh1.3:80.64
nāpyapa vādi kayā |
nor with [the wrong view] of depreciating [what does exist];
sbh1.3:80.65
asantaṁ dharmaṁ na samāropayati |
what is not the Dharma he does not superimpose [onto Dharma],
sbh1.3:80.66
santaṁ dharmaṁ nāpavadati |
what is the Dharma he does not depreciate.
sbh1.3:80.67
sa yattadbhavati |
Whatever there is
sbh1.3:80.68
kathitaṁ kātheyaṁ, civā hnā kṣaraṁ, cihnapadavyaṁjanaṁ, lokāyatapratisaṁyuktaṁ |
of speech and chatter — letters, signs, words, [and] phrases connected with worldly doctrine —
sbh1.3:80.69
tannirarthakamiti |
[he recognizes:] ‘that is meaningless,’
sbh1.3:80.70
viditvā ārātparivarjayati |
and knowing this, he avoids it from afar;
sbh1.3:80.71
na te na dayate, na tena prīyate, tena cātmadaṁ vā nyī cī varaṁ dhārayati |
[Damaged/Conjectural]: he does not delight in it, does not take pleasure in it; on this account he wears a self-disciplining robe.
The printed/root tena cātmadaṁ vā nyī cī varaṁ dhārayati is visibly corrupt, but cīvaraṁ dhārayati is recoverable. The English gives the likely functional sense, 'wears a self-disciplining robe,' treating the phrase as related to self-taming (ātma-dama) and marking the line conjectural/corrupt rather than preserving the unresolved vow/robe slash hedge.
sbh1.3:80.72
gṛhasthaiḥ sārdhaṁ, saṁsargaṁ parivarjayanti yati |
Association with householders he avoids —
sbh1.3:80.73
kleśavarddhanaṁ |
[as] defilement-increasing;
sbh1.3:80.74
āryaiḥ saha saṁ sargaṁ karoti |
with the noble he associates —
sbh1.3:80.75
jñānaviśodhakaṁ |
[as] knowledge-purifying.
sbh1.3:80.76
na ca mitrakulānikaroti |
He does not make [overly close] friend-families,
sbh1.3:80.77
pratigṛhṇāti |
[nor] accept [such ties],
sbh1.3:80.78
sāme tato nidānaṁ bhaviṣyatyanekaparyāyeṇa vyākṣepād vyāpāro vā, teṣāmvā punarvipariṇāmādanyathībhāvādutpatsyante śokaparidevaduḥkhadaurmanasyopāyāsā iti |
[knowing:] ‘on account of which there will arise, in many modes, scatter [and] busyness; or, through their further change, [their] becoming otherwise, will arise sorrow, lamentation, suffering, dejection, distress.’
sbh1.3:80.79
utpannotpannāṁśca kleśopakleśānnādhivāsayati |
And the defilements and secondary defilements as they arise he does not accommodate,
sbh1.3:80.80
prajahāti, vinodayati, vyantīkaroti |
[but] abandons, dispels, [and] puts an end to [them],
sbh1.3:80.81
sā tan me ato nidānamutpadyate dṛṣṭadhārmikaṁ vā duḥkhaṁ, sāmparāyikaṁ veti |
[thinking:] ‘lest from that, on that account, there arise either present-life suffering or future-life suffering.’
sbh1.3:80.82
śraddhodayaṁ ca na vinipātayati |
And he does not let the [donors’] arising of faith fall away [through misconduct].
sbh1.3:80.83
acyutaśīlaḥ |
Of unbroken virtue,
sbh1.3:80.84
abhraṣṭavrataḥ, śraddhādeyaṁ paribhuṅkte |
of unfallen vow, he enjoys what is given in faith,
sbh1.3:80.85
na ca śraddhādeyaṁ pratikṣipati |
and he does not reject what is given in faith;
sbh1.3:80.86
na śikṣāṁ pratyākhyāti |
[nor] does he reject the training.
sbh1.3:80.87
ātmadoṣāntaraskhalitagaveṣī vā bhavati |
He is a seeker of his own faults and slip-ups,
sbh1.3:80.88
praticchannakalyāṇo vivṛtapāpakaḥ |
a concealer of [his own] good, a revealer of [his own] evil.
sbh1.3:80.89
paradoṣāntaskhaliteṣu nābhogaḥ saṁvidya te cāpattirnāpadyate |
In others’ faults and slip-ups he does not engage; he does not fall into transgression,
sbh1.3:80.90
jīvitahetorapi |
[not] even for the sake of [his] life;
sbh1.3:80.91
āpannaśca laghu laghveva yathādharmaṁ pratikaroti |
and if fallen, lightly very lightly he makes amends according to Dharma.
sbh1.3:80.92
itikaraṇīyeṣu ca dakṣo bhavatyanalasaḥ, svayaṁkārī, na parataḥ kāyaparicaryāṁ paryeṣate |
In duties to be done he is skilled, non-lazy, a self-doer, not seeking bodily service from others.
sbh1.3:80.93
buddhānāṁ ca buddhaśrāvakāṇāṁ ca, acintyamanubhāvaṁ, gaṁbhīrāṁ ca deśanāmadhimucyate |
And he has confident conviction in the inconceivable greatness of the Buddhas and Buddha-disciples, and in the deep teaching,
sbh1.3:80.94
na pratikṣipati |
[and] does not reject [it]:
sbh1.3:80.95
tathāgatā eva janakāḥ paśyakā draṣṭāro nāhamiti |
‘The Tathāgatas alone are the knowers, seers, beholders — not I.’
sbh1.3:80.96
dṛṣṭvā na ca svayaṁ dṛṣṭiparāmarśasthāyī bhavatyasamaṁjasagrāhī, duḥpratiniḥsargamaṁntrī |
Having seen thus, he does not himself become fixed in view-clinging, a wrong-grasper, [or] one whose counsel is hard to relinquish.
sbh1.3:80.97
sa ebhirguṇairyuktaḥ, evaṁ vihārī, evaṁ śikṣamāṇaḥ, śramaṇālaṁkāreṇālaṁkṛtaḥ śobhate, tadyathā kaścideva puruṣaḥ yuvā, mananajātīyaḥ, kāmopabhogī, snātānuliptaḥ |
He, endowed with these qualities, so dwelling, so training, adorned with the ornament of the recluse, shines — just as some young man of vital nature, an enjoyer of sense-pleasures, bathed and anointed,
The closing simile — the well-adorned young man — picks up and inverts the ornament-simile from 12.22–29 (where virtue is contrasted with worldly ornaments that fail in old age and misfortune): there, worldly ornaments fail and only virtue endures; here, the worldly ornament succeeds as a comparison-vehicle precisely because the disciple's recluse-ornament is its true, non-decaying analogue. The two passages function as inclusio at the opening and close of the saṁbhāra-section.
sbh1.3:80.98
avavādavastuprāvṛtaḥ |
[Damaged/Conjectural: clothed in clean garments],
The printed/root avavādavastuprāvṛtaḥ is likely corrupt in the ornament simile. The surrounding sequence is the stock image of a young man 'bathed and anointed, clothed, adorned with ornaments'; the expected phrase is probably avadāta-vastra-prāvṛtaḥ ('clothed in clean/bright garments') or a close equivalent. The previous 'approved [/finely-chosen] clothing' read avavāda as approval/instruction and does not fit the simile. The English is therefore marked conjectural/corrupt.
sbh1.3:80.99
vividhairbhūṣaṇairalaṁkṛtaḥ śobhate |
adorned with various ornaments, shines —
sbh1.3:80.100
tadyathā harṣairvā, keyūrairvā, aṁgulimudrikayā vā, jātarūparajatamālayā vā |
namely with necklaces, bracelets, ring on the finger, or garland of gold and silver —
sbh1.3:80.101
evameva sa vividhaiḥ śramaṇālaṁkārairguṇairalaṁkṛto |
just so he, adorned with the various recluse-ornament qualities,
sbh1.3:80.102
bhāsate, tapati, virocate |
shines, glows, blazes.
sbh1.3:80.103
tasmācchramaṇālaṁkāra ityucyate |
Therefore it is called ornament of the recluse.
sbh1.3:80.104
ayamucyate śramaṇālaṁkāraḥ |
This is called the ornament of the recluse.
sbh1.3:81.1
uddānaṁ |
Summary verse:
sbh1.3:82.1
syādātmaparasampatticchandaḥ śīlendriyastathā |
Endowment-from-self, endowment-from-others, [wholesome dharma-]desire, virtue, sense-faculty [restraint],
The closing uddāna (summary verse) is in śloka meter; English here drops the meter and adds bracketed clarifications so the verse remains a list-pointer rather than a freestanding statement. Verses 82–83 enumerate the twelve saṁbhāras of the chapter (with kalyāṇamitratā implicit under parasampat); verses 84–85 enumerate the qualities of the recluse-ornament treated in sections 73–80.
sbh1.3:82.2
bhojanaṁ caiva jāgaryā saṁprajānadvihāritā |
food, wakefulness, dwelling with full awareness,
sbh1.3:83.1
caiva tathā śravaṇacintanā saddharmaśravaṇacintanā |
and likewise hearing-and-reflection on the true dharma,
sbh1.3:83.2
anantarāyastyāgaśca alaṁkāreṇa paścimaḥ |
non-obstacle, generosity, and the ornament [of the recluse] last.
sbh1.3:84.1
śraddhā aśaṭhaḥ alpābādhavīrya prajñā alpecchatā saṁtuṣṭiḥ supoṣatā |
Faith, non-deceit, little affliction, energy, wisdom, few desires, contentment, ease of being cared for,
sbh1.3:85.1
subharatā dhutaprāṇādikamātratā kṣānti ḥ saurabhyapeśalā |
ease of support, the ascetic qualities, pleasingness, knowing-measure, patience, gentleness, loveliness.
sbh1.3:86.1
yogācārabhūmau śrāvakabhūmisaṁgṛhītāyāṁ prathamaṁ yogasthānaṁ |
[Here ends] the first yoga-position, comprised within the Śrāvakabhūmi of the Yogācārabhūmi.
Closing colophon for the entire Yogasthāna I — not just the naiṣkramyabhūmi. The Sanskrit accusative prathamaṁ yogasthānaṁ leaves the verb implicit (samāptam, 'is completed'); rendered here with a bracketed gloss. The same colophon-form repeats at the close of YS II, III, and IV in the source.
